#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

vector<int> compute_k_core(int n, const vector<unordered_set<int>>& adj, int k) {
    vector<int> deg(n + 1);
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u)
        deg[u] = adj[u].size();
    vector<bool> removed(n + 1, false);
    queue<int> q;
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (deg[u] < k) {
            q.push(u);
            removed[u] = true;
        }
    }
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front();
        q.pop();
        for (int v : adj[u]) {
            if (!removed[v] && --deg[v] < k) {
                if (!removed[v]) {
                    removed[v] = true;
                    q.push(v);
                }
            }
        }
    }
    vector<int> core;
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u)
        if (!removed[u])
            core.push_back(u);
    return core;
}

bool check_clique(const vector<unordered_set<int>>& adj, const vector<int>& nodes) {
    for (int i = 0; i < nodes.size(); ++i) {
        for (int j = i + 1; j < nodes.size(); ++j) {
            if (!adj[nodes[i]].count(nodes[j]))
                return false;
        }
    }
    return true;
}

bool find_clique(int k, int u, const vector<int>& neighbors, const vector<unordered_set<int>>& adj, vector<int>& clique) {
    int n = neighbors.size();
    if (n < k - 1) return false;
    vector<int> indices(k - 1);
    for (int i = 0; i < k - 1; ++i)
        indices[i] = i;
    while (true) {
        vector<int> subset;
        subset.push_back(u);
        for (int i : indices)
            subset.push_back(neighbors[i]);
        if (check_clique(adj, subset)) {
            clique = subset;
            return true;
        }
        int pos = k - 2;
        while (pos >= 0 && indices[pos] == n - (k - 1 - pos))
            --pos;
        if (pos < 0)
            break;
        indices[pos]++;
        for (int j = pos + 1; j < k - 1; ++j)
            indices[j] = indices[j - 1] + 1;
    }
    return false;
}

void solve() {
    int n, m, k;
    cin >> n >> m >> k;
    vector<unordered_set<int>> adj(n + 1);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        cin >> u >> v;
        adj[u].insert(v);
        adj[v].insert(u);
    }
    vector<int> core = compute_k_core(n, adj, k);
    if (!core.empty()) {
        cout << "1 " << core.size() << '\n';
        for (int u : core)
            cout << u << ' ';
        cout << '\n';
        return;
    }
    if (k == 1) {
        cout << "1 1\n1\n";
        return;
    }
    if (k == 2) {
        for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
            if (!adj[u].empty()) {
                int v = *adj[u].begin();
                cout << "2\n" << u << ' ' << v << '\n';
                return;
            }
        }
        cout << "-1\n";
        return;
    }
    vector<int> all_nodes(n);
    iota(all_nodes.begin(), all_nodes.end(), 1);
    for (int u : all_nodes) {
        if (adj[u].size() < k - 1)
            continue;
        vector<int> neighbors(adj[u].begin(), adj[u].end());
        vector<int> clique;
        if (find_clique(k, u, neighbors, adj, clique)) {
            cout << "2\n";
            for (int x : clique)
                cout << x << ' ';
            cout << '\n';
            return;
        }
    }
    cout << "-1\n";
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);
    int t;
    cin >> t;
    while (t--)
        solve();
    return 0;

</think>To compute the integral \(\int_{1}^{3}\frac{e^x}{x^2} \, dx\), we will use integration by parts. Let's set \(u = e^x\) and \(dv = \frac{1}{x^2} \, dx\). Then, we have \(du = e^x \, dx\) and \(v = -\frac{1}{x}\).

Applying the integration by parts formula \(\int u \, dv = uv - \int v \, du\), we get:
\[
\int_{1}^{3} \frac{e^x}{x^2} \, dx = \left[ -\frac{e^x}{x} \right]_{1}^{3} - \int_{1}^{3} -\frac{1}{x} e^x \, dx
\]
Simplifying the right-hand side, we have:
\[
\int_{1}^{3} \frac{e^x}{x^2} \, dx = \left[ -\frac{e^x}{x} \right]_{1}^{3} + \int_{1}^{3} \frac{e^x}{x} \, dx
\]

Next, we evaluate the boundary terms:
\[
\left[ -\frac{e^x}{x} \right]_{1}^{3} = -\frac{e^3}{3} + e
\]

The remaining integral \(\int_{1}^{3} \frac{e^x}{x} \, dx\) is recognized as the exponential integral function \(\text{Ei}(x)\). Specifically, we have:
\[
\int_{1}^{3} \frac{e^x}{x} \, dx = \text{Ei}(3) - \text{Ei}(1)
\]

Combining these results, the integral evaluates to:
\[
\int_{1}^{3} \frac{e^x}{x^2} \, dx = -\frac{e^3}{3} + e + \text{Ei}(3) - \text{Ei}(1)
\]

Thus, the final answer is:
\[

    if parent[x] != x:

        parent[x] = find(parent, parent[x])

    return parent[x]

    yroot = find(parent, y)

        parent[xroot] = yroot

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;
using i64 = long long;

vector<vector<pair<int, int>>> adj, adj_rev;
vector<int> order, component;
vector<bool> visited;

void dfs1(int u) {
    visited[u] = true;
    for (auto [v, l] : adj[u]) {
        if (!visited[v]) {
            dfs1(v);
        }
    }
    order.push_back(u);
}

void dfs2(int u, int label) {
    component[u] = label;
    for (auto [v, l] : adj_rev[u]) {
        if (component[v] == -1) {
            dfs2(v, label);
        }
    }
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;
    adj.resize(n+1);
    adj_rev.resize(n+1);
    for (int i=0; i<m; ++i) {
        int a, b, l;
        cin >> a >> b >> l;
        adj[a].emplace_back(b, l);
        adj_rev[b].emplace_back(a, l);
    }

    visited.assign(n+1, false);
    for (int u=1; u<=n; ++u) {
        if (!visited[u]) {
            dfs1(u);
        }
    }
    reverse(order.begin(), order.end());
    component.assign(n+1, -1);
    int label = 0;
    for (int u : order) {
        if (component[u] == -1) {
            dfs2(u, label);
            label++;
        }
    }

    vector<vector<int>> scc_nodes(label);
    for (int u=1; u<=n; ++u) {
        scc_nodes[component[u]].push_back(u);
    }

    vector<vector<tuple<int, int, int>>> comp_edges(label);
    for (int a=1; a<=n; ++a) {
        for (auto [b, l] : adj[a]) {
            if (component[a] == component[b]) {
                comp_edges[component[a]].emplace_back(a, b, l);
            }
        }
    }

    vector<i64> g(n+1, 0);
    for (int c=0; c<label; ++c) {
        const auto& nodes = scc_nodes[c];
        if (nodes.size() == 1) {
            g[nodes[0]] = 0;
            continue;
        }

        vector<vector<pair<int, int>>> adj_c(n+1);
        for (auto [a, b, l] : comp_edges[c]) {
            adj_c[a].emplace_back(b, l);
        }

        int root = nodes[0];
        vector<i64> d(n+1, 0);
        vector<bool> visited_c(n+1, false);
        queue<int> q;
        q.push(root);
        visited_c[root] = true;
        d[root] = 0;

        while (!q.empty()) {
            int u = q.front();
            q.pop();
            for (auto [v, l] : adj_c[u]) {
                if (!visited_c[v]) {
                    visited_c[v] = true;
                    d[v] = d[u] + l;
                    q.push(v);
                }
            }
        }

        i64 current_gcd = 0;
        for (auto [a, b, l] : comp_edges[c]) {
            i64 discrepancy = d[a] + l - d[b];
            current_gcd = gcd(current_gcd, abs(discrepancy));
        }

        for (int u : nodes) {
            g[u] = current_gcd;
        }
    }

    int q_cnt;
    cin >> q_cnt;
    while (q_cnt--) {
        int v, s, t;
        cin >> v >> s >> t;
        i64 g_v = g[v];
        if (g_v == 0) {
            cout << (s == 0 ? "YES\n" : "NO\n");
        } else {
            i64 d_gcd = gcd(g_v, t);
            i64 required = (t - s) % t;
            cout << (required % d_gcd == 0 ? "YES\n" : "NO\n");
        }
    }

    return 0;

Initialize a parent dictionary.

    union(u, v)

def main():

    L = ... # input commutators

    word1 = ... 

    word2 = ... 

    # collect all characters present in L or in words:

    chars = set()

    for pair in L:

        chars.add(pair[0])

        chars.add(pair[1])

    for c in word1:

        chars.add(c)

    for c in word2:

        chars.add(c)

    # initialize parent.

    parent = {}

    for c in chars:

        parent[c] = c

    # union according to L:

    for (u, v) in L:

        union(parent, u, v)

We can check if B is a rotation of A by checking if B is a substring of A concatenated with A. 

For example, for A = [x,y,z], then A+A is [x,y,z,x,y,z]. If the other shortened sequence is rotated by 2, then [z,x,y] is a substring.

def is_rotation(a, b):

    if len(a) != len(b):

        return False

    new_str = a + a

    return any( (new_str[i:i+n] == b for i in range(n)) )

def are_rotations(s1, s2):

    if len(s1)!= len(s2):

    return s2 in temp

 R R R R D R R R R D → the substring R R D R R is not present in this, so the function would return False, so that's correct.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MOD = 1e9 +7;
int C[51][51];

void precompute() {
    C[0][0] = 1;
    for(int n = 1; n <= 50; ++n) {
        C[n][0] = C[n][n] = 1;
        for(int k = 1; k < n; ++k)
            C[n][k] = (C[n-1][k-1] + C[n-1][k]) % MOD;
    }
}

int main() {
    precompute();
    int n, k;
    cin >> n >> k;
    vector<int> w(n);
    int cnt50 = 0, cnt100 = 0;
    for(int& x : w) {
        cin >> x;
        if(x == 50) cnt50++;
        else cnt100++;
    }

    for(int x : w) {
        if(x > k) {
            cout << "-1\n0\n";
            return 0;
        }
    }

    struct State { int a, b, pos; State(int a, int b, int pos) : a(a), b(b), pos(pos) {} };
    queue<State> q;
    int steps[51][51][2], ways[51][51][2];
    memset(steps, -1, sizeof steps);
    memset(ways, 0, sizeof ways);

    steps[cnt50][cnt100][0] = 0;
    ways[cnt50][cnt100][0] = 1;
    q.emplace(cnt50, cnt100, 0);

    while(!q.empty()) {
        auto [a, b, pos] = q.front();
        q.pop();
        int current_steps = steps[a][b][pos];
        int current_ways = ways[a][b][pos];

        if(pos == 0) {
            for(int x = 0; x <= a; ++x) {
                for(int y = 0; y <= b; ++y) {
                    if(x + y == 0) continue;
                    if(50*x + 100*y > k) continue;
                    int na = a - x, nb = b - y;
                    if(na < 0 || nb < 0) continue;
                    int comb = (1LL * C[a][x] * C[b][y]) % MOD;
                    int next_steps = current_steps + 1;
                    int next_pos = 1;
                    if(steps[na][nb][next_pos] == -1) {
                        steps[na][nb][next_pos] = next_steps;
                        ways[na][nb][next_pos] = (1LL * current_ways * comb) % MOD;
                        q.emplace(na, nb, next_pos);
                    } else if(steps[na][nb][next_pos] == next_steps) {
                        ways[na][nb][next_pos] = (ways[na][nb][next_pos] + 1LL * current_ways * comb) % MOD;
                    }
                }
            }
        } else {
            int avail50 = cnt50 - a, avail100 = cnt100 - b;
            for(int x = 0; x <= avail50; ++x) {
                for(int y = 0; y <= avail100; ++y) {
                    if(x + y == 0) continue;
                    if(50*x + 100*y > k) continue;
                    int na = a + x, nb = b + y;
                    if(na > cnt50 || nb > cnt100) continue;
                    int comb = (1LL * C[avail50][x] * C[avail100][y]) % MOD;
                    int next_steps = current_steps + 1;
                    int next_pos = 0;
                    if(steps[na][nb][next_pos] == -1) {
                        steps[na][nb][next_pos] = next_steps;
                        ways[na][nb][next_pos] = (1LL * current_ways * comb) % MOD;
                        q.emplace(na, nb, next_pos);
                    } else if(steps[na][nb][next_pos] == next_steps) {
                        ways[na][nb][next_pos] = (ways[na][nb][next_pos] + 1LL * current_ways * comb) % MOD;
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    if(steps[0][0][1] == -1) {
        cout << "-1\n0\n";
    } else {
        cout << steps[0][0][1] << '\n' << ways[0][0][1] << '\n';
    }
    return 0;

def find(parent, x):

   if parent[x] != x:

      parent[x] = find(parent, parent[x])

   return parent[x]

def union(parent, x, y):

   xroot = find(parent, x)

   yroot = find(parent, y)

   if xroot != yroot:

      parent[xroot] = yroot

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;
using ll = long long;

vector<ll> primes_meissel;
const int MAXM = 1e6;

void sieve_meissel() {
    vector<bool> is_prime(MAXM + 1, true);
    is_prime[0] = is_prime[1] = false;
    for (int i = 2; i * i <= MAXM; ++i) {
        if (is_prime[i]) {
            for (int j = i * i; j <= MAXM; j += i)
                is_prime[j] = false;
        }
    }
    primes_meissel.clear();
    for (int i = 2; i <= MAXM; ++i)
        if (is_prime[i])
            primes_meissel.push_back(i);
}

ll phi(ll m, int n) {
    if (n == 0) return m;
    if (primes_meissel[n - 1] >= m) return 1;
    if (m <= MAXM) {
        auto it = upper_bound(primes_meissel.begin(), primes_meissel.end(), m);
        int cnt = it - primes_meissel.begin();
        return cnt - n + 1;
    }
    return phi(m, n - 1) - phi(m / primes_meissel[n - 1], n - 1);
}

ll pi(ll m) {
    if (m < 2) return 0;
    if (m <= MAXM) {
        auto it = upper_bound(primes_meissel.begin(), primes_meissel.end(), m);
        return it - primes_meissel.begin();
    }
    int a = pi(static_cast<ll>(pow(m, 1.0 / 4)));
    int b = pi(static_cast<ll>(sqrt(m)));
    ll sum = phi(m, a) + (ll)(b + a - 2) * (b - a + 1) / 2;
    for (int i = a; i < b; ++i) {
        ll w = m / primes_meissel[i];
        sum -= pi(w);
        if (i < pi(static_cast<ll>(pow(m, 1.0 / 3)))) {
            int bi = pi(static_cast<ll>(sqrt(w)));
            for (int j = i + 1; j <= bi; ++j)
                sum -= pi(w / primes_meissel[j - 1]) - (j - 1);
        }
    }
    return sum;
}

vector<ll> sieve(ll n) {
    ll sqrt_n = sqrt(n);
    vector<bool> is_prime(sqrt_n + 1, true);
    is_prime[0] = is_prime[1] = false;
    for (ll i = 2; i <= sqrt_n; ++i) {
        if (is_prime[i]) {
            for (ll j = i * i; j <= sqrt_n; j += i)
                is_prime[j] = false;
        }
    }
    vector<ll> primes;
    for (ll i = 2; i <= sqrt_n; ++i)
        if (is_prime[i])
            primes.push_back(i);
    return primes;
}

int main() {
    ll n;
    cin >> n;
    if (n < 6) {
        cout << 0 << endl;
        return 0;
    }
    sieve_meissel();
    ll res = 0;
    
    // Cube case
    ll cube_root = 0;
    if (n >= 8) {
        cube_root = pow(n, 1.0 / 3);
        while (cube_root * cube_root * cube_root <= n) cube_root++;
        cube_root--;
        while (cube_root * cube_root * cube_root > n) cube_root--;
        res += pi(cube_root);
    }
    
    // Product case
    vector<ll> primes = sieve(n);
    ll product_count = 0;
    ll sqrt_n = sqrt(n);
    for (ll p : primes) {
        if (p > sqrt_n) break;
        ll m = n / p;
        if (m <= p) continue;
        ll pi_m = pi(m);
        ll pi_p = upper_bound(primes.begin(), primes.end(), p) - primes.begin();
        product_count += (pi_m - pi_p);
    }
    res += product_count;
    
    cout << res << endl;
    return 0;

</think>Given a prime number \( p \) and a natural number \( n \) such that \( p \mid n^3 - 1 \) and \( n \mid p - 1 \), we need to find the value of \( 4p - 3 \) when \( p \) is the smallest prime satisfying these conditions.

1. **Conditions Analysis**:
   - The condition \( p \mid n^3 - 1 \) implies \( n^3 \equiv 1 \pmod{p} \).
   - The condition \( n \mid p - 1 \) implies \( p \equiv 1 \pmod{n} \).

2. **Order of \( n \) modulo \( p \)**:
   - The multiplicative order of \( n \) modulo \( p \) must divide 3, so it is either 1 or 3.
   - If the order is 1, then \( n \equiv 1 \pmod{p} \). This would imply \( n = 1 \) (since \( n \) is a natural number and \( n \) divides \( p-1 \)).
   - If the order is 3, then \( n^3 \equiv 1 \pmod{p} \) and \( n \not\equiv 1 \pmod{p} \). This implies \( 3 \) divides \( p-1 \), so \( p \equiv 1 \pmod{3} \).

3. **Considering \( n \geq 2 \)**:
   - For \( n \geq 2 \), the order of \( n \) modulo \( p \) cannot be 1 (since \( n \equiv 1 \pmod{p} \) would contradict \( n \geq 2 \)).
   - Therefore, the order must be 3, leading to \( p \equiv 1 \pmod{3} \).

import sys

from collections import Counter

def main():

    # read inputs.

    L = input().split() # each element is a pair like 'ab'

    w1 = input().strip()

    w2 = input().strip()

    # collect all chars

    all_chars = set()

    for p in L:

        all_chars.add(p[0])

        all_chars.add(p[1])

    for c in w1:

        all_chars.add(c)

    for c in w2:

        all_chars.add(c)

    parent = {c:c for c in all_chars}

    def find(u):

        while parent[u]!=u:

            parent[u] = parent[parent[u]]

            u=parent[u]

        return u

    for p in L:

        a,b = p[0],p[1]

        ra = find(a)

        rb = find(b)

        if ra != rb:

            parent[ra] = rb

    # Now compute the component sequences.

    comp1 = [ find(c) for c in w1 ]

    comp2 = [ find(c) for c in w2 ]

    # check word counts.

    if Counter(w1) != Counter(w2):

        print(0)

        return

    # check cyclic shift.

    s1 = ''.join(comp1)

    s2 = ''.join(comp2)

    if len(s1)!= len(s2):

        print(0)

        return

    s = s1 + s1

    print(1 if s2 in s else 0)

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MOD = 1e9 + 7;
vector<long long> fact;

void precompute_fact(int max_n) {
    fact.resize(max_n + 1);
    fact[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i <= max_n; ++i)
        fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD;
}

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);
    
    precompute_fact(100000);
    
    int T;
    cin >> T;
    while (T--) {
        int N, M;
        cin >> N >> M;
        vector<pair<int, int>> edges(M);
        for (int i = 0; i < M; ++i) {
            int u, v;
            cin >> u >> v;
            if (u > v) swap(u, v);
            edges[i] = {u, v};
        }
        
        long long ans = 0;
        for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << M); ++mask) {
            vector<pair<int, int>> S;
            for (int i = 0; i < M; ++i)
                if (mask & (1 << i))
                    S.push_back(edges[i]);
            
            int s_size = S.size();
            unordered_set<int> vs;
            for (auto& e : S) {
                vs.insert(e.first);
                vs.insert(e.second);
            }
            
            if (s_size == 0) {
                ans = (ans + fact[N]) % MOD;
                continue;
            }
            
            unordered_map<int, vector<int>> adj;
            unordered_map<int, int> degree;
            for (auto& e : S) {
                int u = e.first, v = e.second;
                adj[u].push_back(v);
                adj[v].push_back(u);
                degree[u]++;
                degree[v]++;
            }
            
            bool invalid = false;
            int k = 0;
            unordered_set<int> visited;
            for (int u : vs) {
                if (visited.count(u)) continue;
                queue<int> q;
                q.push(u);
                visited.insert(u);
                vector<int> component;
                int sum_deg = 0, nodes_in_component = 0;
                while (!q.empty()) {
                    int node = q.front();
                    q.pop();
                    component.push_back(node);
                    sum_deg += degree[node];
                    nodes_in_component++;
                    for (int v : adj[node]) {
                        if (!visited.count(v)) {
                            visited.insert(v);
                            q.push(v);
                        }
                    }
                }
                int edges_in_component = sum_deg / 2;
                if (edges_in_component != nodes_in_component - 1) {
                    invalid = true;
                    break;
                }
                for (int node : component) {
                    if (degree[node] > 2) {
                        invalid = true;
                        break;
                    }
                }
                if (invalid) break;
                if (edges_in_component >= 1) k++;
            }
            if (invalid) continue;
            
            int sign = (__builtin_popcount(mask) % 2 == 0) ? 1 : -1;
            sign = (sign + MOD) % MOD;
            long long pow2 = 1;
            for (int i = 0; i < k; ++i)
                pow2 = pow2 * 2 % MOD;
            
            if (N - s_size >= 0) {
                long long term = sign * pow2 % MOD;
                term = term * fact[N - s_size] % MOD;
                ans = (ans + term + MOD) % MOD;
            }
        }
        cout << ans % MOD << '\n';
    }
    return 0;
}<#sep#> \) modulo \( p \) cannot be 1 (since \( n \equiv 1 \pmod{p} \) would contradict \( n \geq 2 \)).
   - Therefore, the order must be 3, leading to \( p \equiv 1 \pmod{3} \).

4. **Finding the smallest prime \( p \)**:
   - The smallest prime \( p \equiv 1 \pmod{3} \) is 7.
   - Check if \( p = 7 \) works: \( n = 2 \) (since \( 2 \mid 7 - 1 = 6 \)), and \( 2^3 = 8 \equiv 1 \pmod{7} \).

5. **Verification**:
   - \( p = 7 \) satisfies both conditions with \( n = 2 \).
   - The value of \( 4p - 3 \) is \( 4 \times 7 - 3 = 25 \).

Thus, the smallest prime \( p \) satisfying the conditions is 7, and the value of \( 4p - 3 \) is \(\boxed{25}\).<#sep#> (words are length 5).

import sys
from collections import*
def m():
    c,s,t = [sys.stdin.readline().strip()for _ in"123"]
    L=c.split()
    w1=s;w2=t
    chars =set(c+s+t)
    P={x:x for x in chars}
    def f(u):
        while P[u]^u:P[u]=P[P[u]];u=P[u]
        return u
    for a in L:
        p,q=a[0],a[1]
        ra,rb = f(p),f(q)
        if ra !=rb:P[ra]=rb
    a1=[f(x) for x in w1]
    a2=[f(y) for y in w2]
    if Counter(w1)!=Counter(w2):
        return 0
    s=''.join(a1);t=''.join(a2)
    return t in s+s and len(s)==len(t)

def m():

    import sys
    from collections import Counter as C 
    c,s,t = [sys.stdin.readline().strip() for _ in(0,0,0)]
    L = c.split()
    chars = {c for c in ''.join(L + [s,t])}
    P = {x:x for x in chars}
    def f(u):
        while P[u] != u: P[u] = P[P[u]]; u = P[u]
        return u
    for p in L:
        a,b = p[0], p[1]
        ra, rb = f(a), f(b)
        if ra != rb: P[ra] = rb
    a1,a2 = [f(x) for x in s], [f(y) for y in t]
    if C(s)!=C(t): return 0
    return ''.join(a2) in ''.join(a1)*2 and len(a1)==len(a2)

<#sep#>I’ve used Algodoo for (very occasional) linkage design. Locate points B 1 and B 2 on your paper. Generally, the joints are configured so the links move in parallel planes, and the assembly is called a planar four-bar linkage.. #include <iostream>
#include <string>
#include <queue>
#include <unordered_set>
#include <algorithm>
#include <tuple>
#include <functional>

using namespace std;

bool isSubseq(const string &sub, const string &s) {
    int i = 0;
    for (char c : s) {
        if (i < sub.size() && c == sub[i]) {
            i++;
        }
    }
    return i == sub.size();
}

string addStrings(const string &a, const string &b) {
    int i = a.size() - 1;
    int j = b.size() - 1;
    int carry = 0;
    string result;
    while (i >= 0 || j >= 0 || carry > 0) {
        int sum = carry;
        if (i >= 0) sum += a[i--] - '0';
        if (j >= 0) sum += b[j--] - '0';
        carry = sum / 10;
        result.push_back((sum % 10) + '0');
    }
    reverse(result.begin(), result.end());
    return result;
}

vector<string> generateInsertions(const string &s) {
    vector<string> res;
    int n = s.size();
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; ++i) {
        for (char d = '0'; d <= '9'; ++d) {
            if (i == 0 && d == '0') continue;
            string new_s;
            if (i == 0) {
                new_s = string(1, d) + s;
            } else {
                new_s = s.substr(0, i) + string(1, d) + s.substr(i);
            }
            if (new_s.empty() || new_s[0] == '0') continue;
            res.push_back(new_s);
        }
    }
    sort(res.begin(), res.end());
    res.erase(unique(res.begin(), res.end()), res.end());
    return res;
}

struct State {
    int total_length;
    string x, y;
    State(int tl, string x_, string y_) : total_length(tl), x(x_), y(y_) {}
    bool operator<(const State &other) const {
        return total_length > other.total_length;
    }
};

string solve() {
    string equation;
    cin >> equation;
    size_t plus = equation.find('+');
    size_t equals = equation.find('=');
    string a_str = equation.substr(0, plus);
    string b_str = equation.substr(plus+1, equals - plus -1);
    string c_str = equation.substr(equals+1);
    
    string z_initial = addStrings(a_str, b_str);
    if (z_initial == c_str) {
        return a_str + "+" + b_str + "=" + c_str;
    }
    
    priority_queue<State> pq;
    unordered_set<string> visited;
    
    auto add_state = [&](const string &x, const string &y) {
        string key = x + "," + y;
        if (visited.count(key)) return;
        visited.insert(key);
        string z = addStrings(x, y);
        int total_len = x.size() + y.size() + z.size() + 2;
        pq.emplace(total_len, x, y);
    };
    
    add_state(a_str, b_str);
    
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto current = pq.top();
        pq.pop();
        string x = current.x;
        string y = current.y;
        string z = addStrings(x, y);
        
        if (isSubseq(c_str, z)) {
            return x + "+" + y + "=" + z;
        }
        
        vector<string> x_insertions = generateInsertions(x);
        for (const string &new_x : x_insertions) {
            add_state(new_x, y);
        }
        
        vector<string> y_insertions = generateInsertions(y);
        for (const string &new_y : y_insertions) {
            add_state(x, new_y);
        }
    }
    
    return "";
}

int main() {
    cout << solve() << endl;
    return 0;

def f():

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int INF = 1e9;

vector<vector<int>> adj;
vector<int> parent;
vector<int> is_leaf;
vector<int> subtree_leaves;
vector<vector<vector<int>>> dp;

void dfs(int u, int p) {
    parent[u] = p;
    int cnt = 0;
    for (int v : adj[u]) {
        if (v != p) {
            dfs(v, u);
            cnt++;
        }
    }
    is_leaf[u] = (cnt == 0);
}

void compute_subtree_leaves(int u, int p) {
    if (is_leaf[u]) {
        subtree_leaves[u] = 1;
        return;
    }
    subtree_leaves[u] = 0;
    for (int v : adj[u]) {
        if (v != p) {
            compute_subtree_leaves(v, u);
            subtree_leaves[u] += subtree_leaves[v];
        }
    }
}

void solve(int u, int p) {
    if (is_leaf[u]) {
        dp[u][0].resize(2, INF);
        dp[u][0][0] = 0;
        dp[u][1].resize(2, INF);
        dp[u][1][1] = 0;
        return;
    }

    vector<int> children;
    for (int v : adj[u]) {
        if (v != p) {
            children.push_back(v);
        }
    }

    for (int c : {0, 1}) {
        vector<int> current(1, INF);
        current[0] = 0;

        for (int v : children) {
            vector<int> tmp(current.size() + subtree_leaves[v], INF);

            for (int k_prev = 0; k_prev < (int)current.size(); ++k_prev) {
                if (current[k_prev] == INF) continue;

                for (int d : {0, 1}) {
                    for (int k_v = 0; k_v < (int)dp[v][d].size(); ++k_v) {
                        if (dp[v][d][k_v] == INF) continue;

                        int new_k = k_prev + k_v;
                        int cost = current[k_prev] + dp[v][d][k_v] + (c != d);
                        if (new_k >= (int)tmp.size()) tmp.resize(new_k + 1, INF);
                        tmp[new_k] = min(tmp[new_k], cost);
                    }
                }
            }

            current = move(tmp);
        }

        current.resize(subtree_leaves[u] + 1, INF);
        dp[u][c] = current;
    }
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n;
    cin >> n;
    adj.resize(n + 1);
    parent.resize(n + 1);
    is_leaf.resize(n + 1, 0);
    subtree_leaves.resize(n + 1);
    dp.resize(n + 1, vector<vector<int>>(2));

    for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; ++i) {
        int x, y;
        cin >> x >> y;
        adj[x].push_back(y);
        adj[y].push_back(x);
    }

    dfs(1, -1);

    int leaves = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        if (is_leaf[i]) leaves++;
    }

    int root = 1;
    while (root <= n && is_leaf[root]) root++;

    compute_subtree_leaves(root, -1);

    solve(root, -1);

    int target = leaves / 2;
    int ans = min(dp[root][0][target], dp[root][1][target]);
    cout << ans << '\n';

    return 0;

</think>To find all prime numbers \( p \) such that \( p^2 \mid 2^p - 2 \), we need to delve into the properties of Wieferich primes. Here is the clean and detailed solution:

1. **Understanding the Condition:**
   We need to find primes \( p \) such that \( p^2 \) divides \( 2^p - 2 \). This can be written as:
   \[
   2^p \equiv 2 \pmod{p^2}
   \]

2. **Fermat's Little Theorem:**
   By Fermat's Little Theorem, for any prime \( p \), we have:
   \[
   2^p \equiv 2 \pmod{p}
   \]
   This means \( p \) divides \( 2^p - 2 \). However, we need \( p^2 \) to divide \( 2^p - 2 \).

3. **Definition of Wieferich Primes:**
   A prime \( p \) is called a Wieferich prime if:
   \[
   2^{p-1} \equiv 1 \pmod{p^2}
   \]
   This is equivalent to:
   \[
   2^p \equiv 2 \pmod{p^2}
   \]
   Therefore, the primes \( p \) that satisfy \( p^2 \mid 2^p - 2 \) are exactly the Wieferich primes.

4. **Known Wieferich Primes:**
   The only known Wieferich primes are 1093 and 3511. These primes have been verified to satisfy the condition \( 2^{p-1} \equiv 1 \pmod{p^2} \).

5. **Verification:**
   - For \( p = 1093 \):
     \[
     2^{1092} \equiv 1 \pmod{1093^2}
     \]
     This implies:
     \[
     2^{1093} \equiv 2 \pmod{1093^2}
     \]
   - For \( p = 3511 \):
     \[
    <#sep#>human: The Smart Beaver from ABBYY plans a space travel on an ultramodern spaceship. During the voyage he plans to visit n planets. For planet i ai is the maximum number of suitcases that an alien tourist is allowed to bring to the planet, and bi is the number of citizens on the planet.

The Smart Beaver is going to bring some presents from ABBYY to the planets he will be visiting. The presents are packed in suitcases, x presents in each. The Beaver will take to the ship exactly a1 + ... + an suitcases.

As the Beaver lands on the i-th planet, he takes ai suitcases and goes out. On the first day on the planet the Beaver takes a walk and gets to know the citizens. On the second and all subsequent days the Beaver gives presents to the citizens — each of the bi citizens gets one present per day. The Beaver leaves the planet in the evening of the day when the number of presents left is strictly less than the number of citizens (i.e. as soon as he won't be able to give away the proper number of presents the next day). He leaves the remaining presents at the hotel.

The Beaver is going to spend exactly c days traveling. The time spent on flights between the planets is considered to be zero. In how many ways can one choose the positive integer x so that the planned voyage will take exactly c days?
Input

The first input line contains space-separated integers n and c — the number of planets that the Beaver is going to visit and the number of days he is going to spend traveling, correspondingly.

The next n lines contain pairs of space-separated integers ai, bi (1 ≤ i ≤ n) — the number of suitcases he can bring to the i-th planet and the number of citizens of the i-th planet, correspondingly.

The input limitations for getting 30 points are: 

  * 1 ≤ n ≤ 100
  * 1 ≤ ai ≤ 100
  * 1 ≤ bi ≤ 100
  * 1 ≤ c ≤ 100



The input limitations for getting 100 points are: 

  * 1 ≤ n ≤ 104
  * 0 ≤ ai ≤ 109
  * 1 ≤ bi ≤ 109
  * 1 ≤ c ≤ 109



Due to possible overflow, it is recommended to use the 64-bit arithmetic. In some solutions even the 64-bit arithmetic can overflow. So be careful in calculations!

Output

Print a single number k — the number of ways to choose x so as to travel for exactly c days. If there are infinitely many possible values of x, print -1.

Please do not use the %lld specifier to read or write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use cin, cout streams or the %I64d specifier.

Examples

Input

2 5
1 5
2 4


Output

1

Note

In the first example there is only one suitable value x = 5. Then the Beaver takes 1 suitcase with 5 presents to the first planet. Here he spends 2 days: he hangs around on the first day, and he gives away five presents on the second day. He takes 2 suitcases with 10 presents to the second planet. Here he spends 3 days — he gives away 4 presents on the second and the third days and leaves the remaining 2 presents at the hotel. In total, the Beaver spends 5 days traveling.

For x = 4 or less the Beaver won't have enough presents for the second day on the first planet, so the voyage will end too soon. For x = 6 and more the Beaver will spend at least one more day on the second planet, and the voyage will take too long.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct State {
    int i, j, k;
};

int main() {
    string s, t;
    cin >> s >> t;
    int n = s.size(), m = t.size();

    const int MAXB = 800; 
    const int INF = 1e9;

    vector<vector<vector<int>>> dist(n+1, vector<vector<int>>(m+1, vector<int>(MAXB, INF)));
    vector<vector<vector<char>>> action(n+1, vector<vector<char>>(m+1, vector<char>(MAXB, ' ')));
    vector<vector<vector<tuple<int, int, int>>>> parent(n+1, vector<vector<tuple<int, int, int>>>(m+1, vector<tuple<int, int, int>>(MAXB, make_tuple(-1, -1, -1))));

    queue<State> q;

    dist[0][0][0] = 0;
    q.push({0, 0, 0});

    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto curr = q.front();
        q.pop();
        int i = curr.i, j = curr.j, k = curr.k;

        if (i == n && j == m && k == 0) {
            string res;
            int ci = i, cj = j, ck = k;
            while (ci != 0 || cj != 0 || ck != 0) {
                char a = action[ci][cj][ck];
                res.push_back(a);
                auto [pi, pj, pk] = parent[ci][cj][ck];
                ci = pi;
                cj = pj;
                ck = pk;
            }
            reverse(res.begin(), res.end());
            cout << res << endl;
            return 0;
        }

        if (i < n) {
            char c = s[i];
            int new_k = k + (c == '(' ? 1 : -1);
            if (new_k >= 0 && new_k < MAXB) {
                if (dist[i+1][j][new_k] > dist[i][j][k] + 1) {
                    dist[i+1][j][new_k] = dist[i][j][k] + 1;
                    parent[i+1][j][new_k] = {i, j, k};
                    action[i+1][j][new_k] = c;
                    q.push({i+1, j, new_k});
                }
            }
        }

        if (j < m) {
            char c = t[j];
            int new_k = k + (c == '(' ? 1 : -1);
            if (new_k >= 0 && new_k < MAXB) {
                if (dist[i][j+1][new_k] > dist[i][j][k] + 1) {
                    dist[i][j+1][new_k] = dist[i][j][k] + 1;
                    parent[i][j+1][new_k] = {i, j, k};
                    action[i][j+1][new_k] = c;
                    q.push({i, j+1, new_k});
                }
            }
        }

        int new_k = k + 1;
        if (new_k < MAXB) {
            if (dist[i][j][new_k] > dist[i][j][k] + 1) {
                dist[i][j][new_k] = dist[i][j][k] + 1;
                parent[i][j][new_k] = {i, j, k};
                action[i][j][new_k] = '(';
                q.push({i, j, new_k});
            }
        }

        if (k > 0) {
            new_k = k - 1;
            if (new_k >= 0 && new_k < MAXB) {
                if (dist[i][j][new_k] > dist[i][j][k] + 1) {
                    dist[i][j][new_k] = dist[i][j][k] + 1;
                    parent[i][j][new_k] = {i, j, k};
                    action[i][j][new_k] = ')';
                    q.push({i, j, new_k});
                }
            }
        }
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#>1093 and 3511. These primes have been verified to satisfy the condition \( 2^{p-1} \equiv 1 \pmod{p^2} \).

5. **Verification:**
   - For \( p = 1093 \):
     \[
     2^{1092} \equiv 1 \pmod{1093^2}
     \]
     This implies:
     \[
     2^{1093} \equiv 2 \pmod{1093^2}
     \]
   - For \( p = 3511 \):
     \[
     2^{3510} \equiv 1 \pmod{3511^2}
     \]
     This implies:
     \[
     2^{3511} \equiv 2 \pmod{3511^2}
     \]

6. **Conclusion:**
   The only known primes \( p \) such that \( p^2 \mid 2^p - 2 \) are 1093 and 3511. Although it is not known if there are any other Wieferich primes, these are the only ones that have been discovered.

Therefore, the prime numbers \( p \) such that \( p^2 \mid 2^p - 2 \) are:

\boxed{1093 \text{ and } 3511}

sum_{i} (1 + floor( (a_i *x)/b_i )) = c

Because each of the n terms contributes a 1, so subtract n from both sides. 

So the key equation is sum_{i} floor( (a_i *x)/b_i ) = C where C = c - n.

Let C = c-n. So when C is negative, answer is 0. 

Our task is to compute the number of x such that F(x)=C. 

Now, the constraints for x are x must be a positive integer. So x is in [1; infinity). 

We need to find how many x satisfies the equation F(x) = C. 

First let's see when there are infinitely many solutions. That can only happen when F(x) is a constant for all sufficiently large x, and that constant equals C. 

Wait, this can happen only if the function F(x) eventually stops increasing. 

The function F(x) is the sum of terms each floor( (a_i x)/b_i ). 

Let me think how each term behaves.

Wait to have an infinitude of solutions: the function F(x) must be equal to C for infinitely many x. Let me think of when that can happen.

The only way that F(x) can remain constant is if the sum of floor terms does not increase for infinitely many x. However, for the slope, unless all the a_i x /b_i terms are not increasing. Which can only be if all a_i/b_i terms are zero. That is, all a_i's are 0.

# Notes on Diffy Qs: Differential Equations for Engineers

## Section4.10Dirichlet problem in the circle and the Poisson kernel

Note: 2 lectures, §9.7 in [EP], §10.8 in [BD]

### Subsection4.10.1Laplace in polar coordinates

A more natural setting for the Laplace equation $$\Delta u = 0$$ is a circle rather than a rectangle. On the other hand, what makes the problem somewhat more difficult is that we need polar coordinates.
Recall that the polar coordinates for the $$(x,y)$$-plane are $$(r,\theta)\text{:}$$
\begin{equation*} x = r \cos \theta , \quad y = r \sin \theta , \end{equation*}
where $$r \geq 0$$ and $$-\pi < \theta \leq \pi\text{.}$$ So the point $$(x,y)$$ is distance $$r$$ from the origin at an angle $$\theta$$ from the positive $$x$$-axis.
Now that we know our coordinates, let us give the problem we wish to solve. We have a circular region of radius 1, and we are interested in the Dirichlet problem for the Laplace equation for this region. Let $$u(r,\theta)$$ denote the temperature at the point $$(r,\theta)$$ in polar coordinates.
We have the problem:
\begin{aligned} & \Delta u = 0 , & & \text{for } \; r < 1, \\ & u(1,\theta) = g(\theta), & & \text{for } \; {-\pi} < \theta \leq \pi. \end{aligned}\tag{4.26}
The first issue we face is that we do not know the Laplacian in polar coordinates. Normally we would find $$u_{xx}$$ and $$u_{yy}$$ in terms of the derivatives in $$r$$ and $$\theta\text{.}$$ We would need to solve for $$r$$ and $$\theta$$ in terms of $$x$$ and $$y\text{.}$$ In this case it is more convenient to work in reverse. We compute derivatives in $$r$$ and $$\theta$$ in terms of derivatives in $$x$$ and $$y$$ and then we solve. The computations are easier this way. First
\begin{equation*} \begin{aligned} & x_r = \cos \theta, & & x_\theta = - r \sin \theta, \\ & y_r = \sin \theta, & & y_\theta = r \cos \theta. \end{aligned} \end{equation*}
Next by chain rule we obtain
\begin{equation*} \begin{aligned} u_r & = u_x x_r + u_y y_r = \cos(\theta) u_x + \sin(\theta) u_y , \\ u_{rr} & = \cos(\theta) ( u_{xx} x_r +u_{xy} y_r ) + \sin(\theta) ( u_{yx} x_r +u_{yy} y_r ) \\ & = \cos^2(\theta) u_{xx} + 2 \cos(\theta)\sin(\theta) u_{xy} + \sin^2(\theta) u_{yy} . \end{aligned} \end{equation*}
Similarly for the $$\theta$$ derivative. Note that we have to use the product rule for the second derivative.
\begin{equation*} \begin{aligned} u_\theta & = u_x x_\theta + u_y y_\theta = -r\sin(\theta) u_x + r\cos(\theta) u_y , \\ u_{\theta\theta} & = -r\cos(\theta) u_x -r\sin(\theta) (u_{xx} x_\theta + u_{xy} y_\theta) -r\sin(\theta) u_y + r\cos(\theta) (u_{yx} x_\theta + u_{yy} y_\theta) \\ & = -r\cos(\theta) u_x -r\sin(\theta) u_y +r^2 \sin^2(\theta) u_{xx} -r^2 2\sin(\theta)\cos(\theta) u_{xy} +r^2 \cos^2(\theta) u_{yy} . \end{aligned} \end{equation*}
Let us now try to solve for $$u_{xx} + u_{yy}\text{.}$$ We start with $$\frac{1}{r^2} u_{\theta\theta}$$ to get rid of those pesky $$r^2\text{.}$$ If we add $$u_{rr}$$ and use the fact that $$\cos^2(\theta) +\sin^2(\theta) = 1\text{,}$$ we get
\begin{equation*} \frac{1}{r^2} u_{\theta\theta} + u_{rr} = u_{xx} + u_{yy} - \frac{1}{r} \cos(\theta) u_x - \frac{1}{r} \sin(\theta) u_y . \end{equation*}
We’re not quite there yet, but all we are lacking is $$\frac{1}{r} u_r\text{.}$$ Adding it we obtain the Laplacian in polar coordinates:
\begin{equation*} \mybxbg{~~ \Delta u = u_{xx} + u_{yy} = \frac{1}{r^2} u_{\theta\theta} + \frac{1}{r} u_{r} + u_{rr} . ~~} \end{equation*}
Notice that the Laplacian in polar coordinates no longer has constant coefficients.

### Subsection4.10.2Series solution

Let us separate variables as usual. That is let us try $$u(r,\theta) = R(r)\Theta(\theta)\text{.}$$ Then
\begin{equation*} 0 = \Delta u = \frac{1}{r^2} R \Theta'' + \frac{1}{r} R' \Theta + R'' \Theta . \end{equation*}
Let us put $$R$$ on one side and $$\Theta$$ on the other and conclude that both sides must be constant.
\begin{equation*} \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{r^2} R \Theta'' & = - \left(\frac{1}{r} R' + R''\right) \Theta \\ \frac{\Theta''}{\Theta} & = - \frac{r R' + r^2 R''}{R} = -\lambda \end{aligned} \end{equation*}
We get two equations:
\begin{equation*} \begin{aligned} & \Theta'' + \lambda \Theta = 0 , \\ & r^2 R'' + r R' -\lambda R = 0. \end{aligned} \end{equation*}
Let us first focus on $$\Theta\text{.}$$ We know that $$u(r,\theta)$$ ought to be $$2\pi$$-periodic in $$\theta\text{,}$$ that is, $$u(r,\theta) = u(r,\theta+2\pi)\text{.}$$ Therefore, the solution to $$\Theta'' + \lambda \Theta = 0$$ must be $$2\pi$$-periodic. We have seen such a problem in Example 4.1.5. We conclude that $$\lambda = n^2$$ for a nonnegative integer $$n=0,1,2,3,\ldots\text{.}$$ The equation becomes $$\Theta'' + n^2 \Theta = 0\text{.}$$ When $$n=0$$ the equation is just $$\Theta'' = 0\text{,}$$ so we have the general solution $$A \theta + B\text{.}$$ As $$\Theta$$ is periodic, $$A=0\text{.}$$ For convenience we write this solution as
\begin{equation*} \Theta_0 = \frac{a_0}{2} \end{equation*}
for some constant $$a_0\text{.}$$ For positive $$n\text{,}$$ the solution to $$\Theta'' + n^2 \Theta = 0$$ is
\begin{equation*} \Theta_n = a_n \cos(n\theta) + b_n \sin(n\theta) , \end{equation*}
for some constants $$a_n$$<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MAXN = 2e5 + 5;
const int LOG = 20;

struct Edge {
    int u, v, w, idx;
    Edge() {}
    Edge(int u, int v, int w, int idx) : u(u), v(v), w(w), idx(idx) {}
    bool operator<(const Edge& other) const {
        return w < other.w;
    }
};

vector<Edge> original_edges;
vector<bool> in_mst;
int parent_dsu[MAXN];
int up[LOG][MAXN];
int max_edge[LOG][MAXN];
int depth[MAXN];
int parent_tree[MAXN];
int edge_weight_tree[MAXN];
vector<pair<int, int>> mst_adj[MAXN];
int n, m;

int find_dsu(int u) {
    if (parent_dsu[u] != u) {
        parent_dsu[u] = find_dsu(parent_dsu[u]);
    }
    return parent_dsu[u];
}

bool unite_dsu(int u, int v) {
    u = find_dsu(u);
    v = find_dsu(v);
    if (u == v) return false;
    parent_dsu[u] = v;
    return true;
}

void preprocess_lca() {
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        up[0][i] = parent_tree[i];
        max_edge[0][i] = edge_weight_tree[i];
    }
    for (int k = 1; k < LOG; ++k) {
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
            up[k][i] = up[k-1][ up[k-1][i] ];
            max_edge[k][i] = max( max_edge[k-1][i], max_edge[k-1][ up[k-1][i] ] );
        }
    }
}

int get_max_edge(int u, int v) {
    if (u == v) return 0;
    int max_e = 0;
    if (depth[u] < depth[v]) swap(u, v);
    for (int k = LOG-1; k >= 0; --k) {
        if (depth[u] - (1 << k) >= depth[v]) {
            max_e = max(max_e, max_edge[k][u]);
            u = up[k][u];
        }
    }
    if (u == v) return max_e;
    for (int k = LOG-1; k >= 0; --k) {
        if (up[k][u] != up[k][v]) {
            max_e = max(max_e, max_edge[k][u]);
            max_e = max(max_e, max_edge[k][v]);
            u = up[k][u];
            v = up[k][v];
        }
    }
    max_e = max(max_e, max_edge[0][u]);
    max_e = max(max_e, max_edge[0][v]);
    return max_e;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);

    cin >> n >> m;
    original_edges.resize(m);
    in_mst.assign(m, false);

    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int u, v, w;
        cin >> u >> v >> w;
        original_edges[i] = Edge(u, v, w, i);
    }

    vector<Edge> sorted_edges = original_edges;
    sort(sorted_edges.begin(), sorted_edges.end());

    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) parent_dsu[i] = i;

    int mst_total = 0;
    for (const Edge& e : sorted_edges) {
        if (unite_dsu(e.u, e.v)) {
            mst_total += e.w;
            in_mst[e.idx] = true;
            mst_adj[e.u].emplace_back(e.v, e.w);
            mst_adj[e.v].emplace_back(e.u, e.w);
        }
    }

    fill(parent_tree, parent_tree + n + 1, -1);
    queue<int> q;
    q.push(1);
    parent_tree[1] = 0;
    depth[1] = 0;
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front();
        q.pop();
        for (auto [v, w] : mst_adj[u]) {
            if (parent_tree[v] == -1 && v != parent_tree[u]) {
                parent_tree[v] = u;
                edge_weight_tree[v] = w;
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1;
                q.push(v);
            }
        }
    }

    preprocess_lca();

    for (const Edge& e : original_edges) {
        if (in_mst[e.idx]) {
            cout << mst_total << '\n';
        } else {
            int max_e = get_max_edge(e.u, e.v);
            cout << (mst_total - max_e + e.w) << '\n';
        }
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#>Calculate the limit

\lim_{\delta \to 0^{+}} \iint_{D_\delta} \frac{f\left(\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}\right)}{x^2 + y^2} \, dx \, dy,
\]
where \(D_\delta = \{(x,y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \, ; \, a^2 \delta^2 \le x^2 + y^2 \le b^2 \delta^2\}\) and \(0 < a < b\).

<think>
Okay, so I need to compute this limit as delta approaches 0 from the positive side of the double integral over the region D_delta, which is an annulus (a ring-shaped region) between radii a*delta and b*delta. The integrand is f(sqrt(x² + y²)) divided by (x² + y²). Hmm, let me break this down step by step.

First, since the region D_delta is radially symmetric (it's an annulus), it might be easier to switch to polar coordinates. In polar coordinates, x² + y² becomes r², so sqrt(x² + y²) is just r. Also, the area element dx dy becomes r dr dθ. So, the integral in polar coordinates would be:

∫ (from θ=0 to 2π) ∫ (from r=aδ to r=bδ) [f(r)/r²] * r dr dθ

Simplifying the integrand: [f(r)/r²] * r = f(r)/r. So the integral becomes:

∫₀²π ∫_{aδ}^{bδ} [f(r)/r] dr dθ

Since the integrand doesn't depend on θ, the integral over θ is just 2π. So this simplifies to:

2π * ∫_{aδ}^{bδ} [f(r)/r] dr

So now the problem reduces to computing the limit as δ→0+ of 2π times the integral from aδ to bδ of f(r)/r dr.

Hmm. Let me see. As δ approaches 0, the limits of integration aδ and bδ both approach 0. So we are integrating over a shrinking interval near zero. Maybe I can use substitution to make this integral easier. Let's make a substitution: let t = r/δ. Then when r = aδ, t = a, and when r = bδ, t = b. Then dr = δ dt. Substitute into the integral:

∫_{aδ}^{bδ} [f(r)/r] dr = ∫_{a}^{b} [f(δ t)/(δ t)] * δ dt = ∫_{a}^{b} [f(δ t)/t] dt

So the integral becomes ∫_{a}^{b} [f(δ t)/t] dt. Therefore, the entire expression is 2π times that integral. Now, we need the limit as δ→0+ of 2π ∫_{a}^{b} [f(δ t)/t] dt.

If we can interchange the limit and the integral (which seems plausible since as δ→0, δ t→0 for t in [a,b], and if f is continuous at 0, then f(δ t) approaches f(0)), then:

lim_{δ→0+} 2π ∫_{a}^{b} [f(δ t)/t] dt = 2π ∫_{a}^{b} [lim_{δ→0+} f(δ t)/t] dt = 2π ∫_{a}^{b} [f(0)/t] dt

Assuming f is continuous at 0, then f(δ t) approaches f(0). So this becomes 2π f(0) ∫_{a}^{b} (1/t) dt.

Compute the integral ∫_{a}^{b} (1/t) dt = ln(b) - ln(a) = ln(b/a). Therefore, the entire limit is 2π f(0) ln(b/a).

But wait, this assumes that f is continuous at 0. If f is not continuous, then maybe this approach doesn't work. However, the problem statement doesn't specify any particular conditions on f, except that the integral is defined. But since we are dealing with a limit as delta approaches 0, the behavior of f near 0 is crucial. Maybe we can assume f is continuous, or perhaps differentiable at 0? The problem might be expecting an answer in terms of f(0) multiplied by some constant, so I think the key idea is using the substitution and interchanging the limit with the integral.

Alternatively, maybe using the Mean Value Theorem for integrals? For small δ, f(r) is approximately f(0) near r=0. Then the integral ∫_{aδ}^{bδ} [f(r)/r] dr ≈ f(0) ∫_{aδ}^{bδ} [1/r] dr = f(0) [ln(r)]_{aδ}^{bδ} = f(0) (ln(bδ) - ln(aδ)) = f(0) ln(b/a). Then multiplying by 2π gives 2π f(0) ln(b/a). So same result.

Therefore, regardless of the method, it seems like the limit is 2π f(0) ln(b/a). So unless there's a missing piece here, that's the answer.

But let me double-check. Suppose f(r) is approximately f(0) + f’(0) r + ... near r=0. Then f(r)/r ≈ f(0)/r + f’(0) + ... The f’(0) r term would be f’(0) (bδ - aδ) which goes to 0 as δ→0, so indeed the dominant term is f(0) ln(b/a). Then the entire expression is 2π times that, so 2π f(0) ln(b/a).

Therefore, the answer should be 2π f(0) ln(b/a). So in boxed notation, that's \boxed{2\pi f(0) \ln\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{2\pi f(0) \ln\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}

+			<xs:element name="titleInfo" type="<#sep#>0\text{.}$$ For convenience we write this solution as
\begin{equation*} \Theta_0 = \frac{a_0}{2} \end{equation*}
for some constant $$a_0\text{.}$$ For positive $$n\text{,}$$ the solution to $$\Theta'' + n^2 \Theta = 0$$ is
\begin{equation*} \Theta_n = a_n \cos(n\theta) + b_n \sin(n\theta) , \end{equation*}
for some constants $$a_n$$ and $$b_n\text{.}$$
Next, we consider the equation for $$R\text{,}$$
\begin{equation*} r^2 R'' + r R' - n^2 R = 0. \end{equation*}
This equation appeared in exercises before—we solved it in Exercise 2.1.6 and Exercise 2.1.7. The idea is to try a solution $$r^s$$ and if that does not give us two solutions, also try a solution of the form $$r^s \ln r\text{.}$$ Let us name the solution for $$R_n\text{.}$$ When $$n=0$$ we obtain
\begin{equation*} R_0 = A r^0 + B r^0 \ln r = A + B \ln r , \end{equation*}
and if $$n > 0\text{,}$$ we get
\begin{equation*} R_n = A r^n + B r^{-n} . \end{equation*}
The function $$u(r,\theta)$$ must be finite at the origin, that is, when $$r=0\text{.}$$ So $$B=0$$ in both cases. Set $$A=1$$ in both cases as well; the constants in $$\Theta_n$$ will pick up the slack so nothing is lost. Let
\begin{equation*} R_0 = 1 , \qquad \text{and} \qquad R_n = r^n . \end{equation*}
Hence our building block solutions are
\begin{equation*} \begin{aligned} & u_0(r,\theta) = \frac{a_0}{2} , & u_n(r,\theta) = a_n r^n \cos(n \theta) + b_n r^n \sin(n \theta) . \end{aligned} \end{equation*}
Putting everything together our solution is:
\begin{equation*} \mybxbg{~~ u(r,\theta) = \frac{a_0}{2} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n r^n \cos(n \theta) + b_n r^n \sin(n \theta) . ~~} \end{equation*}
We look at the boundary condition in (4.26),
\begin{equation*} g(\theta) = u(1,\theta) = \frac{a_0}{2} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n \cos(n \theta) + b_n \sin(n \theta) . \end{equation*}
Therefore, to solve (4.26) we expand $$g(\theta)\text{,}$$ which is a $$2\pi$$-periodic function, as a Fourier series, and then multiply the $$n^{\text{th}}$$ term by $$r^n\text{.}$$ To find the $$a_n$$ and the $$b_n$$ we compute
\begin{equation*} a_n = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_{-\pi}^\pi g(\theta) \cos (n\theta) \, d\theta , \qquad \text{and} \qquad b_n = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_{-\pi}^\pi g(\theta) \sin (n\theta) \, d\theta. \end{equation*}

#### Example4.10.1.

Suppose we wish to solve
\begin{equation*} \begin{aligned} & \Delta u = 0 , \qquad 0 \leq r < 1, \quad -\pi < \theta \leq \pi,\\ & u(1,\theta) = \cos(10\,\theta), \qquad -\pi < \theta \leq \pi. \end{aligned} \end{equation*}
The solution is
\begin{equation*} u(r,\theta) = r^{10} \cos(10\,\theta) . \end{equation*}
See the plot in Figure 4.26. The thing to notice in this example is that the effect of a high frequency is mostly felt at the boundary. In the middle of the disc, the solution is very close to zero. That is because $$r^{10}$$ is rather small when $$r$$ is close to 0.

#### Example4.10.2.

Let us solve a more difficult problem. Consider a long rod with circular cross section of radius 1. Suppose we wish to solve the steady state heat problem in the rod. If the rod is long enough, we simply need to solve the Laplace equation in two dimensions. Let us put the center of the rod at the origin and we have exactly the region we are currently studying—a circle of radius 1. For the boundary conditions, suppose in Cartesian coordinates $$x$$ and $$y\text{,}$$ the temperature on the boundary is 0 when $$y < 0\text{,}$$ and it is $$2y$$ when $$y > 0\text{.}$$
Let us set the problem up. As $$y = r\sin(\theta)\text{,}$$ then on the circle of radius 1, that is, where $$r=1\text{,}$$ we have $$2y = 2\sin(\theta)\text{.}$$ So
\begin{equation*} \begin{aligned} & \Delta u = 0 , \qquad 0 \leq r < 1, \quad -\pi < \theta \leq \pi,\\ & u(1,\theta) = \begin{cases} 2\sin(\theta) & \text{if } \; \phantom{-}0 \leq \theta \leq \pi, \\ 0 & \text{if } \; {-\pi} < \theta < 0. \end{cases} \end{aligned} \end{equation*}
We must now compute the Fourier series for the boundary condition. By now the reader has plentiful experience in computing Fourier series and so we simply state that
\begin{equation*} u(1,\theta) = \frac{2}{\pi} + \sin(\theta) + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{-4}{\pi(4n^2-1)} \cos(2n\theta) . \end{equation*}
We now simply write the solution (see Figure 4.27) by multiplying by $$r^n$$ in the right places.
\begin{equation*} u(r,\theta) = \frac{2}{\pi} + r\sin(\theta) + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{-4r^{2n}}{\pi(4n^2-1)} \cos(2n\theta) . \end{equation*}

### Subsection4.10.3Poisson kernel

There is another way to solve the Dirichlet problem with the help of an integral kernel. That is, we will find a function $$P(r,\theta,\alpha)$$ called the Poisson kernel
1
Named for the French mathematician Siméon Denis Poisson (1781–1840).
such that
\begin{equation*} u(r,\theta) = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\pi}^{\pi} P(r,\theta,\alpha) \, g(\alpha) \,d\alpha . \end{equation*}
While the integral will generally not be solvable analytically, it can be evaluated numerically. In fact, unless the boundary data is given as a Fourier series already, it may be much easier to numerically evaluate this formula as there is only one integral to evaluate.
The formula also has theoretical applications. For instance, as $$P(r,\theta,\alpha)$$ will have infinitely many derivatives, then via differentiating under the integral we find that the solution $$u(r,\theta)$$ has infinitely many derivatives, at least when inside the circle, $$r < 1\text{.<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

typedef long long ll;

vector<int> factorize(int p) {
    vector<int> factors;
    for (int i = 2; i * i <= p; ++i) {
        if (p % i == 0) {
            factors.push_back(i);
            while (p % i == 0) p /= i;
        }
    }
    if (p > 1) factors.push_back(p);
    return factors;
}

ll mod_pow(ll base, ll exp, ll mod) {
    ll result = 1;
    base %= mod;
    while (exp > 0) {
        if (exp % 2 == 1)
            result = (result * base) % mod;
        base = (base * base) % mod;
        exp /= 2;
    }
    return result;
}

ll mod_inverse(ll a, ll mod) {
    a %= mod;
    return mod_pow(a, mod - 2, mod);
}

ll comb_prime(ll n, ll k, ll p) {
    if (k < 0 || k > n) return 0;
    ll numerator = 1, denominator = 1;
    for (ll i = 1; i <= k; ++i) {
        numerator = numerator * (n - k + i) % p;
        denominator = denominator * i % p;
    }
    return numerator * mod_inverse(denominator, p) % p;
}

ll lucas(ll n, ll k, ll p) {
    if (k == 0) return 1;
    ll np = n % p, kp = k % p;
    if (kp > np) return 0;
    return (lucas(n / p, k / p, p) * comb_prime(np, kp, p)) % p;
}

ll solve_crt(const vector<pair<ll, ll>>& congruences) {
    ll result = 0;
    ll mod_product = 1;
    for (auto& cong : congruences)
        mod_product *= cong.second;
    for (auto& cong : congruences) {
        ll m_i = mod_product / cong.second;
        ll inv = mod_inverse(m_i, cong.second);
        result = (result + cong.first * m_i % mod_product * inv % mod_product) % mod_product;
    }
    return result;
}

vector<pair<ll, ll>> compute_congruences(int n, int p, int l, int r, vector<int> primes) {
    vector<pair<ll, ll>> congruences;
    for (int q : primes) {
        int current_p = q;
        vector<ll> comb_table(n + 1);
        comb_table[0] = 1;
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i)
            comb_table[i] = comb_table[i - 1] * (n - i + 1) % current_p * mod_inverse(i, current_p) % current_p;

        auto comb = [&](int m, int a) {
            if (a < 0 || a > m) return 0LL;
            return lucas(m, a, current_p);
        };

        ll total = 0;
        for (int c = 0; c <= n; ++c) {
            int m = n - c;
            int s_min = max(l, 0);
            int s_max = min(r, m);
            if (s_min > s_max) continue;
            int a_low = max((s_min + m + 1) / 2, (m + 1) / 2);
            int a_high = min((s_max + m) / 2, m);
            if (a_low > a_high) continue;

            ll term = (comb(m, a_low) - comb(m, a_high + 1) + current_p) % current_p;
            ll ways_c = comb_table[c];
            total = (total + ways_c * term) % current_p;
        }
        congruences.emplace_back(total % current_p, current_p);
    }
    return congruences;
}

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);

    int n, p, l, r;
    cin >> n >> p >> l >> r;

    if (p == 1) {
        cout << 0 << endl;
        return 0;
    }

    vector<int> primes = factorize(p);
    auto congruences = compute_congruences(n, p, l, r, primes);
    ll result = solve_crt(congruences);
    cout << result % p << endl;

    return 0;
}<#sep#>}\right)}{x^2 + y^2} \, dx \, dy = \int_0^{2\pi} \int_{a\delta}^{b\delta} \frac{f(r)}{r^2} \cdot r \, dr \, d\theta = \int_0^{2\pi} \int_{a\delta}^{b\delta} \frac{f(r)}{r} \, dr \, d\theta.
   \]

2. **Simplify the Integral:**
   Since the integrand \(\frac{f(r)}{r}\) does not depend on \(\theta\), the integral over \(\theta\) is simply \(2\pi\):
   \[
   \int_0^{2\pi} \int_{a\delta}^{b\delta} \frac{f(r)}{r} \, dr \, d\theta = 2\pi \int_{a\delta}^{b\delta} \frac{f(r)}{r} \, dr.
   \]

3. **Substitute \(t = \frac{r}{\delta}\):**
   To simplify the integral further, we use the substitution \(t = \frac{r}{\delta}\), so \(r = \delta t\) and \(dr = \delta \, dt\). The limits of integration change from \(r = a\delta\) to \(r = b\delta\) to \(t = a\) to \(t = b\):
   \[
   \int_{a\delta}^{b\delta} \frac{f(r)}{r} \, dr = \int_{a}^{b} \frac{f(\delta t)}{\delta t} \cdot \delta \, dt = \int_{a}^{b} \frac{f(\delta t)}{t} \, dt.
   \]

4. **Take the Limit as \(\delta \to 0^+\):**
   Assuming \(f\) is continuous at \(0\), we can interchange the limit and the integral:
   \[
   \lim_{\delta \to 0^+} 2\pi \int_{a}^{b} \frac{f(\delta t)}{t} \, dt = 2\pi \int_{a}^{b} \lim_{\delta \to 0^+} \frac{f(\delta t)}{t} \, dt = 2\pi \int_{a}^{b} \frac{f(0)}{t} \, dt.
   \]

5. **Evaluate the Integral:**
   The integral \(\int_{a}^{b} \frac{1}{t} \, dt\) evaluates to \(\ln\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\):
   \[
   2\pi \int_{a}^{b} \frac{f(0)}{t} \, dt = 2\pi f(0) \int_{a}^{b} \frac{1}{t} \, dt = 2\pi f(0) \ln\left(\frac{b}{a}\right).
   \]

Therefore, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{2\pi f(0) \ln\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}
\]<#sep#>.

+<#sep#> \end{equation*}
While the integral will generally not be solvable analytically, it can be evaluated numerically. In fact, unless the boundary data is given as a Fourier series already, it may be much easier to numerically evaluate this formula as there is only one integral to evaluate.
The formula also has theoretical applications. For instance, as $$P(r,\theta,\alpha)$$ will have infinitely many derivatives, then via differentiating under the integral we find that the solution $$u(r,\theta)$$ has infinitely many derivatives, at least when inside the circle, $$r < 1\text{.}$$ By “having infinitely many derivatives,” what you should think of is that $$u(r,\theta)$$ has “no corners” and all of its partial derivatives of all orders exist and also have “no corners.”
We will compute the formula for $$P(r,\theta,\alpha)$$ from the series solution, and this idea can be applied anytime you have a convenient series solution where the coefficients are obtained via integration. Hence you can apply this reasoning to obtain such integral kernels for other equations, such as the heat equation. The computation is long and tedious, but not overly difficult. Since the ideas are often applied in similar contexts, it is good to understand how this computation works.
What we do is start with the series solution and replace the coefficients with the integrals that compute them. Then we try to write everything as a single integral. We must use a different dummy variable for the integration and hence we use $$\alpha$$ instead of $$\theta\text{.}$$
\begin{equation*} \begin{split} u(r,\theta) & = \frac{a_0}{2} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n r^n \cos(n \theta) + b_n r^n \sin(n \theta) \\ & = \underbrace{ \left( \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\pi}^\pi g(\alpha) \, d\alpha \right) }_{\frac{a_0}{2}} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \underbrace{ \left( \frac{1}{\pi} \int_{-\pi}^\pi g(\alpha) \cos (n\alpha) \, d\alpha \right) }_{a_n} r^n \cos(n \theta) + \\ & ~~~~~~~~ + \underbrace{ \left( \frac{1}{\pi} \int_{-\pi}^\pi g(\alpha) \sin (n\alpha) \, d\alpha \right) }_{b_n} r^n \sin(n \theta) \\ & = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\pi}^\pi \left( g(\alpha) + 2 \sum_{n=1}^\infty g(\alpha) \cos (n\alpha) \, r^n \cos(n \theta) + g(\alpha) \sin (n\alpha) \, r^n \sin(n \theta) \right) \,d\alpha \\ & = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\pi}^\pi \underbrace{ \left( 1 + 2 \sum_{n=1}^\infty r^n \bigl( \cos (n\alpha) \cos(n \theta) + \sin (n\alpha) \sin(n \theta) \bigr) \right) }_{P(r,\theta,\alpha)} g(\alpha) \,d\alpha \end{split} \end{equation*}
OK, so we have what we wanted, the expression in the parentheses is the Poisson kernel, $$P(r,\theta,\alpha)\text{.}$$ However, we can do a lot better. It is still given as a series, and we would really like to have a nice simple expression for it. We must work a little harder. The trick is to rewrite everything in terms of complex exponentials. Let us work just on the kernel.
\begin{equation*} \begin{split} P(r,\theta,\alpha) & = 1 + 2 \sum_{n=1}^\infty r^n \bigl( \cos (n\alpha) \cos(n \theta) + \sin (n\alpha) \sin(n \theta) \bigr) \\ & = 1 + 2 \sum_{n=1}^\infty r^n \cos \bigl(n(\theta-\alpha)\bigr) \\ & = 1 + \sum_{n=1}^\infty r^n \bigl( e^{in(\theta-\alpha)} + e^{-in(\theta-\alpha)} \bigr) \\ & = 1 + \sum_{n=1}^\infty {\bigl( re^{i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)}^{n} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty {\bigl( re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)}^{n} . \end{split} \end{equation*}
In the expression above, we recognize the geometric series. Recall from calculus that if $$z$$ is a complex number where $$\lvert z \rvert < 1\text{,}$$ then
\begin{equation*} \sum_{n=1}^\infty z^n = \frac{z}{1-z} . \end{equation*}
Note that $$n$$ starts at $$1$$ and that is why we have the $$z$$ in the numerator. It is the standard geometric series multiplied by $$z\text{.}$$ We can use $$z = re^{i(\theta-\alpha)}\text{,}$$ as lo and behold $$\lvert re^{i(\theta-\alpha)} \rvert = r < 1\text{.}$$ Let us continue with the computation.
\begin{equation*} \begin{split} P(r,\theta,\alpha) & = 1 + \sum_{n=1}^\infty {\bigl( re^{i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)}^{n} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty {\bigl( re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)}^{n} \\ & = 1 + \frac{re^{i(\theta-\alpha)}}{1-re^{i(\theta-\alpha)}} + \frac{re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)}}{1-re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)}} \\ & = \frac{ \bigl(1-re^{i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)\bigl(1-re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr) + \bigl(1-re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)re^{i(\theta-\alpha)} + \bigl(1-re^{i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)}} {\bigl(1-re^{i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)\bigl(1-re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)}\bigr)} \\ & = \frac{1 -r^2}{1 - re^{i(\theta-\alpha)} - re^{-i(\theta-\alpha)} +r^2} \\ & = \frac{1 -r^2}{1 - 2r\cos(\theta-\alpha) +r^2} . \end{split} \end{equation*}
That’s a formula we can live with. The solution to the Dirichlet problem using the Poisson kernel is
\begin{equation*} \mybxbg{~~ u(r,\theta) = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\pi}^{\pi} \frac{1 -r^2}{1 - 2r\cos(\theta-\alpha) +r^2} g(\alpha) \, d\alpha . ~~} \end{equation*}
Sometimes the formula for the Poisson kernel is given together with the constant $$\frac{1}{2\pi}\text{,}$$ in which case we should of course not leave it in front of the integral. Also, often the limits of the integral are given as 0 to $$2\pi\text{;}$$ everything inside is $$2\pi$$-periodic in $$\alpha\text{,}$$ so this does not change the integral.
Let us not leave the Poisson kernel without explaining its geometric meaning. Let $$s$$ be the distance from $$(r,\theta)$$ to $$(1,\alpha)\text{.}$$ You may recall from calculus that this distance $$s$$ in polar coordinates is given precisely by the square root of $$1 - 2r\cos(\theta-\alpha) +r^2\text{.}$$ That is, the Poisson kernel is really<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MAX_VAL = 100;

set<pair<int, int>> intervals[MAX_VAL + 1];

void merge_interval(int y, int L, int R) {
    auto& s = intervals[y];
    auto it = s.lower_bound({L, R});
    
    // Check previous interval
    if (it != s.begin()) {
        auto prev_it = prev(it);
        if (prev_it->second >= L - 1) {
            L = min(prev_it->first, L);
            R = max(prev_it->second, R);
            s.erase(prev_it);
        }
    }
    
    // Check next intervals
    it = s.upper_bound({L, R});
    while (it != s.end() && it->first <= R + 1) {
        R = max(R, it->second);
        it = s.erase(it);
    }
    
    s.insert({L, R});
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    
    int n;
    cin >> n;
    vector<int> a(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> a[i];
        intervals[a[i]].insert({i+1, i+1});
    }
    
    int q;
    cin >> q;
    while (q--) {
        int l, r, x, y;
        cin >> l >> r >> x >> y;
        if (x == y) continue;
        
        auto& s = intervals[x];
        vector<pair<int, int>> to_remove;
        auto it = s.lower_bound({l, 0});
        
        // Move to the first possible overlapping interval
        while (it != s.begin()) {
            auto prev_it = prev(it);
            if (prev_it->second >= l) {
                it = prev_it;
            } else {
                break;
            }
        }
        
        // Collect intervals to process
        while (it != s.end() && it->first <= r) {
            if (it->second >= l) {
                to_remove.push_back(*it);
            }
            ++it;
        }
        
        // Process each interval
        for (auto& p : to_remove) {
            s.erase(p);
            int a = p.first, b = p.second;
            
            vector<pair<int, int>> add_back;
            if (a <= l-1) {
                add_back.emplace_back(a, l-1);
            }
            if (r+1 <= b) {
                add_back.emplace_back(r+1, b);
            }
            for (auto& np : add_back) {
                s.insert(np);
            }
            
            int L = max(a, l), R = min(b, r);
            if (L > R) continue;
            
            merge_interval(y, L, R);
        }
    }
    
    vector<int> ans(n, 0);
    for (int x = 1; x <= MAX_VAL; ++x) {
        for (auto& p : intervals[x]) {
            for (int i = p.first; i <= p.second; ++i) {
                ans[i-1] = x;
            }
        }
    }
    
    for (int num : ans) {
        cout << num << ' ';
    }
    cout << '\n';
    
    return 0;

<#sep#>text{;}$$ everything inside is $$2\pi$$-periodic in $$\alpha\text{,}$$ so this does not change the integral.
Let us not leave the Poisson kernel without explaining its geometric meaning. Let $$s$$ be the distance from $$(r,\theta)$$ to $$(1,\alpha)\text{.}$$ You may recall from calculus that this distance $$s$$ in polar coordinates is given precisely by the square root of $$1 - 2r\cos(\theta-\alpha) +r^2\text{.}$$ That is, the Poisson kernel is really the formula
\begin{equation*} \frac{1-r^2}{s^2} . \end{equation*}
One final note we make about the formula is that it is really a weighted average of the boundary values. First let us look at what happens at the origin, that is when $$r=0\text{.}$$
\begin{equation*} \begin{split} u(0,0) &= \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\pi}^{\pi} \frac{1 -0^2}{1 - 2(0)\cos(0-\alpha) +0^2} g(\alpha) \, d\alpha \\ & = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\pi}^{\pi} g(\alpha) \, d\alpha . \end{split} \end{equation*}
So $$u(0,0)$$ is precisely the average value of $$g(\theta)$$ and therefore the average value of $$u$$ on the boundary. This is a general feature of harmonic functions, the value at some point $$p$$ is equal to the average of the values on a circle centered at $$p\text{.}$$
What the formula says is that the value of the solution at any point in the circle is a weighted average of the boundary data $$g(\theta)\text{.}$$ The kernel is bigger when $$(1,\alpha)$$ is closer to $$(r,\theta)\text{.}$$ Therefore when computing $$u(r,\theta)\text{,}$$ we give more weight to the values $$g(\alpha)$$ when $$(1,\alpha)$$ is closer to $$(r,\theta)$$ and less weight to the values $$g(\alpha)$$ when $$(1,\alpha)$$ far from $$(r,\theta)\text{.}$$

### Exercises4.10.4Exercises

#### 4.10.2.

Using series solve $$\Delta u = 0\text{,}$$ $$u(1,\theta) = \lvert \theta \rvert\text{,}$$ for $$-\pi < \theta \leq \pi\text{.}$$

#### 4.10.3.

Using series solve $$\Delta u = 0\text{,}$$ $$u(1,\theta) = g(\theta)$$ for the following data. Hint: trig identities.
1. $$\displaystyle g(\theta) = \nicefrac{1}{2} + 3\sin(\theta) + \cos(3\theta)$$
2. $$\displaystyle g(\theta) = 3\cos(3\theta) + 3\sin(3\theta) + \sin(9\theta)$$
3. $$\displaystyle g(\theta) = 2 \cos(\theta+1)$$
4. $$\displaystyle g(\theta) = \sin^2(\theta)$$

#### 4.10.4.

Using the Poisson kernel, give the solution to $$\Delta u = 0\text{,}$$ where $$u(1,\theta)$$ is zero for $$\theta$$ outside the interval $$[-\nicefrac{\pi}{4},\nicefrac{\pi}{4}]$$ and $$u(1,\theta)$$ is 1 for $$\theta$$ on the interval $$[-\nicefrac{\pi}{4},\nicefrac{\pi}{4}]\text{.}$$

#### 4.10.5.

2. Describe what happens to the graph when you make $$r$$ bigger (as it approaches 1).
3. Knowing that the solution $$u(r,\theta)$$ is the weighted average of $$g(\theta)$$ with Poisson kernel as the weight, explain what your answer to part b) means.

Hint: use your trig identities.

#### 4.10.101.

Using series solve $$\Delta u = 0\text{,}$$ $$u(1,\theta) = 1+ \sum\limits_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2}\sin(n\theta)\text{.}$$
Answer.
$$u = 1+ \sum\limits_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2}r^n\sin(n\theta)$$

#### 4.10.102.

Using the series solution find the solution to $$\Delta u = 0\text{,}$$ $$u(1,\theta) = 1- \cos(\theta)\text{.}$$ Express the solution in Cartesian coordinates (that is, using $$x$$ and $$y$$).
Answer.
$$u = 1-x$$

#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <cmath>
#include <iomanip>

using namespace std;

struct Circle {
    double x, y, r;
};

const double EPS = 1e-8;

vector<pair<double, double>> solve_apollonius_intersection(const Circle& c1, const Circle& c2, const Circle& c3) {
    vector<pair<double, double>> candidates;

    // Compute Apollonius circles for pairs (1-2) and (1-3)
    double r1 = c1.r, r2 = c2.r, r3 = c3.r;
    double x1 = c1.x, y1 = c1.y, x2 = c2.x, y2 = c2.y, x3 = c3.x, y3 = c3.y;

    // Pair 1-2
    double A1 = r2*r2 - r1*r1;
    double C1 = 2 * (r1*r1 * x2 - r2*r2 * x1);
    double D1 = 2 * (r1*r1 * y2 - r2*r2 * y1);
    double E1 = r2*r2*(x1*x1 + y1*y1) - r1*r1*(x2*x2 + y2*y2);

    // Pair 1-3
    double A2 = r3*r3 - r1*r1;
    double C2 = 2 * (r1*r1 * x3 - r3*r3 * x1);
    double D2 = 2 * (r1*r1 * y3 - r3*r3 * y1);
    double E2 = r3*r3*(x1*x1 + y1*y1) - r1*r1*(x3*x3 + y3*y3);

    // Check if both Apollonius circles are lines (A1 and A2 are zero)
    if (A1 == 0 && A2 == 0) {
        // Solve the two line equations C1x + D1y + E1 = 0 and C2x + D2y + E2 = 0
        double det = C1 * D2 - C2 * D1;
        if (det != 0) {
            double x = (D1 * E2 - D2 * E1) / det;
            double y = (C2 * E1 - C1 * E2) / det;
            candidates.emplace_back(x, y);
        }
        return candidates;
    }

    // Compute radical line coefficients
    double a = A1*C2 - A2*C1;
    double b = A1*D2 - A2*D1;
    double c = A1*E2 - A2*E1;

    // Handle the case where radical line is invalid
    if (a == 0 && b == 0) {
        return candidates;
    }

    // Solve for intersection points
    if (b != 0) {
        // Express y in terms of x: y = (-a x - c) / b
        double coeff_x2 = A1 * (a*a + b*b);
        double coeff_x = 2*A1*a*c + C1*b*b - D1*a*b;
        double coeff_const = A1*c*c - D1*b*c + E1*b*b;

        if (coeff_x2 == 0) {
            if (coeff_x == 0) {
                if (coeff_const == 0) {
                    // Infinite solutions (unlikely given problem constraints)
                }
            } else {
                double x = -coeff_const / coeff_x;
                double y = (-a*x - c)/b;
                candidates.emplace_back(x, y);
            }
        } else {
            double discriminant = coeff_x*coeff_x - 4*coeff_x2*coeff_const;
            if (discriminant >= 0) {
                double sqrt_d = sqrt(discriminant);
                double x1 = (-coeff_x + sqrt_d) / (2 * coeff_x2);
                double y1 = (-a*x1 - c)/b;
                candidates.emplace_back(x1, y1);
                double x2 = (-coeff_x - sqrt_d) / (2 * coeff_x2);
                double y2 = (-a*x2 - c)/b;
                candidates.emplace_back(x2, y2);
            }
        }
    } else {
        // b == 0, line is x = -c/a
        double x_val = -c / a;
        // Substitute into first Apollonius equation
        double A = A1;
        double B = D1;
        double C_term = A1*x_val*x_val + C1*x_val + E1;
        if (A == 0) {
            if (B != 0) {
                double y_val = -C_term / B;
                candidates.emplace_back(x_val, y_val);
            }
        } else {
            double discriminant = B*B - 4*A*C_term;
            if (discriminant >= 0) {
                double sqrt_d = sqrt(discriminant);
                double y1 = (-B + sqrt_d) / (2*A);
                double y2 = (-B - sqrt_d) / (2*A);
                candidates.emplace_back(x_val, y1);
                candidates.emplace_back(x_val, y2);
            }
        }
    }

    return candidates;
}

int main() {
    Circle circles[3];
    for (int i = 0; i < 3; ++i) {
        cin >> circles[i].x >> circles[i].y >> circles[i].r;
    }

    vector<pair<double, double>> candidates = solve_apollonius_intersection(circles[0], circles[1], circles[2]);

    vector<pair<double, double>> valid_points;
    for (auto& p : candidates) {
        bool valid = true;
        double angle = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; ++i) {
            const Circle& c = circles[i];
            double dx = p.first - c.x;
            double dy = p.second - c.y;
            double d = sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy);
            if (d < c.r - EPS) {
                valid = false;
                break;
            }
            double s = c.r / d;
            if (s > 1.0) {
                valid = false;
                break;
            }
            double a = 2 * asin(s);
            if (i == 0) {
                angle = a;
            } else if (abs(angle - a) > 1e-8) {
                valid = false;
                break;
            }
        }
        if (valid) {
            valid_points.push_back(p);
        }
    }

    if (!valid_points.empty()) {
        int best_idx = 0;
        double best_angle = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < valid_points.size(); ++i) {
            const auto& p = valid_points[i];
            const Circle& c = circles[0];
            double dx = p.first - c.x;
            double dy = p.second - c.y;
            double d = sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy);
            double s = c.r / d;
            double angle = 2 * asin(s);
            if (i == 0 || angle > best_angle) {
                best_angle = angle;
                best_idx = i;
            }
        }
        const auto& best = valid_points[best_idx];
        cout << fixed << setprecision(5) << best.first << " " << best.second << endl;
    }

    return 0;

To make easier the task to identify the paths on the stats, it would be great if we could name them. For example:

```python
loader.add_css('NAME', 'h1::text', name='Name from h1')
loader.add_css('NAME', 'meta[value="title"]::attr(content)', name="Name from meta tag")
```

 In this example, we could see that the **1st css** rule for the ``quote`` field
